Um, well... I guess I sort of mentioned the work stuff earlier, with the inventory and the supply closet. I really hope I'm not repeating myself too much, I know your time is valuable.

Is that normal to feel sometimes?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have a lack of energy most days, even when you haven't done anything particularly strenuous?\n\nPatient: Um, tired? I mean, I guess I get a little tired sometimes, but who doesn't, right? My job keeps me on my feet a lot, so it's probably just that. I definitely don't want to sound lazy or anything. I always make sure I get my work done, and I try to help out around the house as much as I can. \n\nI wouldn't say I have a lack of energy, really. Just the normal amount of tired at the end of the day, I think. That's pretty standard, isn't it? I hope I'm answering this right, I just don't want to make a big deal out of nothing.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve had trouble thinking clearly or concentrating, even outside of work tasks—like when you're reading, watching TV, or making decisions?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess I sort of mentioned the work stuff earlier, with the inventory and the supply closet. I really hope I'm not repeating myself too much, I know your time is valuable. \n\nBut, um, outside of work... I mean, I guess it happens a little bit? Like, I was trying to study for that supervisor certification at home the other night, and I just kept reading the same paragraph over and over again without it really sticking. Is that normal to feel sometimes?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have a lack of energy most days, even when you haven't done anything particularly strenuous?\n\nPatient: Um, tired? I mean, I guess I get a little tired sometimes, but who doesn't, right? My job keeps me on my feet a lot, so it's probably just that. I definitely don't want to sound lazy or anything. I always make sure I get my work done, and I try to help out around the house as much as I can. \n\nI wouldn't say I have a lack of energy, really. Just the normal amount of tired at the end of the day, I think. That's pretty standard, isn't it? I hope I'm answering this right, I just don't want to make a big deal out of nothing.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve had trouble thinking clearly or concentrating, even outside of work tasks—like when you're reading, watching TV, or making decisions?\n\nPatient: Um, well... Like I can't quite piece the steps together as fast as I usually do. \n\nI don't think it's anything terrible, though. Probably just me being a little scattered, you know? I always make sure I get my work done, and I try to help out around the house as much as I can. \n\nI wouldn't say I have a lack of energy, really. Just the normal amount of tired at the end of the day, I think. That's pretty standard, isn't it? I hope I'm answering this right, I just don't want to make a big deal out of nothing.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve had trouble thinking clearly or concentrating, even outside of work tasks—like when you're reading, watching TV, or making decisions?\n\nPatient: Um, well... People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Um... yeah, I guess I'd have to say yes to that. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's just... it's this heavy, flat feeling that kind of follows me around most days. 

Even when nothing bad is happening at all. Like I said, I could just be pushing my cart down an empty hallway at work, just doing my normal routine, or... or even sitting right there with my family, trying my best to act normal and be present. And it's still just... there. Hanging over me.

I really try not to let it show, and I definitely don't want to sound like I'm just feeling sorry for myself when I have so much to be grateful for. Is it helpful that I'm explaining it this way?

Um... well, two weeks? It's... it's actually been a lot longer than that, to be honest. 

I guess... well, it's been going on for about three years now. It kind of started back when the cleaning contractor I work for switched our schedules around. They moved us to these rotating evening and overnight shifts, mostly covering the larger commercial properties. 

I hope it's okay that I didn't mention how long it's been right away? I just... I didn't want to sound like I'm complaining about my job, because I'm really grateful to be working and providing for my family. A lot of guys would love to have the hours, you know? But yeah, that heavy feeling has kind of just been there since that schedule change happened, most days for the last three years. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right timeline.

I still like doing the things I've always liked. \n\nI hope that's an okay answer? I just really don't want you to think I'm, like, neglecting my family or being a bad dad or anything. I still care about all that stuff a lot, I promise. I want to be a good dad and a good employee. Is... is it normal to feel like that sometimes? I hope I'm not making too big a deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've noticed a decrease in your ability to think clearly or concentrate, even when you're not particularly stressed or tired?\n\nPatient: Um, well... But the actual things I love doing, they still feel just as good as they always did. \n\nI hope it's not frustrating that my answer is the same as before? I just want to be totally honest with you, and I wouldn't want you to think I'm losing interest in my kids or my wife. That's not the case at all. Am I explaining that okay?\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that you take less pleasure or interest in most of the things you used to enjoy, even if you still do them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... no, I really don't think so. I mean, I know you just asked about this, and I hope I'm not being difficult by giving the same answer again. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.

I really do still enjoy the things I've always enjoyed. Like I said, fixing up those old radios still gives me a lot of satisfaction. And I still love spending time with my wife and kids. I haven't lost interest in any of that, I promise. It's just that low feeling that's always kind of there, but it doesn't stop me from enjoying my hobbies or my family. 

I'm sorry if it seems like I'm repeating myself. I just want to be sure I'm understanding your questions correctly. Is it okay that my answer is the same? I really want to be helpful and make sure I'm giving you what you need to know.
